```json
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nHybrid linear recurrent architectures---including GatedDeltaNet (GDN), RetNet, HGRN2, Gated Linear Attention (GLA), and DeltaNet---achieve aggregate benchmark scores within 1--2 points of each other at matched parameter budgets, yet they can differ on up to 90\\% of the individual examples they affect. This per-example divergence is invisible in aggregate metrics: architectures that score similarly reach that score by gaining and losing on different inputs, and the choice of backbone carries hidden consequences for which specific examples a deployed system handles correctly.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.56\\textwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\end{minipage}%\n\\hfill\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.42\\textwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig4_jaccard_saturation.pdf}\n\\end{minipage}\n\\caption{Per-example overlap appears stable from minimal attention across benchmarks. (a,\\,b) LAMBADA: cross-architecture $\\sigma$ shows a non-significant decreasing trend at 1.3B (solid; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed), while pairwise Jaccard of helped/hurt sets appears stable from 4\\% attention onward. (c) This pattern holds across all four main benchmarks: mean pairwise $\\bar{J}$ remains flat across attention ratios (all $\\rho \\leq 0.80$, $p \\geq .333$). Shaded bands are illustrative ($n = 3$).}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nThis tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nPrior work on predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} and underspecification \\citep{damour2022underspecification,mccoy2020berts} documents this risk for seed variation within a single architecture, but whether it extends to architectural variation in hybrid recurrent models remains open. If architectures that score similarly in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, the backbone choice carries hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first controlled within-family per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures---GatedDeltaNet (gated delta rules), RetNet (multi-scale exponential decay), and HGRN2 (gated state expansion)---at five attention ratios (0--25\\%) and two scales (340M, 1.3B). We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed categories. On four benchmarks (LAMBADA, $N = 5{,}153$; HellaSwag, $N = 10{,}042$; PIQA, $N = 1{,}838$; ARC-Easy, $N = 2{,}376$) we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap via Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central finding: architectures that appear equivalent by aggregate benchmarks diverge systematically at the per-example level, and this divergence is established by minimal attention.\n\nOur central finding is that architectural variation produces structured predictive multiplicity whose magnitude depends on task type, not attention ratio. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), varies $2\\times$ across task types but only 0.03 within each benchmark across attention ratios; pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across all ten five-architecture pairs). A supporting observation is that per-example overlap appears to stabilize at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat at higher ratios---even on LAMBADA, where aggregate variance shows a non-significant decreasing trend ($\\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; at 1.3B only), per-example Jaccard does not change (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}).\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item Architectural variation in hybrid recurrent models---controlled within a single model zoo sharing training data and hyperparameters---produces structured predictive multiplicity whose magnitude varies by task type (or evaluation format) rather than attention ratio (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}). Five-architecture validation confirms the pattern (\\S\\ref{ssec:robustness}; Appendix~\\ref{app:extended_arch}).\n    \\item Per-example overlap appears to stabilize at the first attention level (4\\%) across all four benchmarks: minimal attention may establish which examples each architecture affects, with no observed homogenization at higher ratios ($n = 5$ ratios; \\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}).\n    \\item Pure-model confidence margins partially predict which examples attention will affect (AUC 0.70--0.74), with rescue thresholds architecture-invariant but disruption thresholds varying 47\\% across architectures---a second dimension of multiplicity beyond per-example disagreement (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}; Appendix~\\ref{app:prediction_framework}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",
        "numLines": 34,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 34
      }
```

```json
        "content": "\\section{Method}\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe describe the model configurations, evaluation benchmarks, and analytical framework used to measure how attention affects cross-architecture agreement at both aggregate and per-example levels.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Model zoo summary \\citep{wang2025hybrid}. All configurations share training data, tokenizer, and hyperparameters; they differ only in backbone and attention ratio.}\n\\label{tab:model_zoo}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}\n\\toprule\nArchitecture & Recurrence mechanism & Scales \\\\\n\\midrule\nGatedDeltaNet & Gated delta rules & 340M, 1.3B \\\\\nRetNet & Multi-scale exp.\\ decay & 340M, 1.3B \\\\\nHGRN2 & Gated state expansion & 340M, 1.3B \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{@{}l@{}}{\\footnotesize Attention ratios: 0\\%, 4\\%, 7.7\\%, 14.3\\%, 25\\%} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{3}{@{}l@{}}{\\footnotesize Extended (340M only): +GLA, +DeltaNet} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Model Zoo}\\label{ssec:model_zoo}\n\nWe use the pretrained model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid} (Table~\\ref{tab:model_zoo}), which provides controlled comparisons: GatedDeltaNet \\citep{yang2024gateddeltanet}, RetNet \\citep{sun2023retnet}, and HGRN2 \\citep{qin2024hgrn2} at five attention ratios and two scales. The GDN 1.3B h3-1 configuration uses 16 attention heads vs.\\ 32 for RetNet and HGRN2 (\\S\\ref{ssec:robustness}). We adopt the h$k$-1 notation: h3-1 = 25\\% attention, h6-1 = 14.3\\%, h12-1 = 7.7\\%, h24-1 = 4\\%. Within each scale, all models share identical training data, tokenizer, and hyperparameters, isolating architecture and attention configuration. The zoo also includes a Transformer baseline at both scales.\n\n\\subsection{Task Taxonomy}\\label{ssec:task_taxonomy}\n\nWe evaluate on 10 benchmarks grouped into four categories defined by the primary cognitive demand of each task. This taxonomy (informal; not pre-registered) was fixed prior to analysis following standard categorizations in the evaluation literature \\citep{arora2024zoology} and is treated as hypothesis-generating rather than confirmatory (see Appendix~\\ref{app:benchmark_details} for the confirmatory/exploratory split), not derived post hoc from observed results. The categories test whether the effect of attention on cross-architecture variance depends on task type.\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Retrieval}: LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada}, which requires predicting a final word from long-range context without reasoning or world knowledge.\n    \\item \\textbf{Comprehension}: Boolean Questions (BoolQ), a reading comprehension task with yes/no answers.\n    \\item \\textbf{Reasoning}: HellaSwag, AI2 Reasoning Challenge (ARC-Challenge; \\citealt{clark2018arc}), WinoGrande, and Physical Intuition Question Answering (PIQA). Choice of Plausible Alternatives (COPA; $n = 100$) is supplementary; its small sample renders $\\alpha$ unreliable (Appendix~\\ref{app:subsample_sensitivity}).\n    \\item \\textbf{Mixed}: ARC-Easy, Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU), SciQ, and XStoryCloze, which combine retrieval, comprehension, and reasoning demands.\n\\end{itemize}\n\nLAMBADA is the only free-form generation benchmark in our evaluation; all others use multiple-choice scoring. Its higher $\\alpha$ (0.30 vs.\\ 0.14--0.21 for multiple-choice tasks) may partly reflect the larger answer space inherent to open-ended prediction, and the multiple-choice format may mechanically constrain per-example divergence by limiting the number of distinct error patterns. We cannot fully disentangle task type from evaluation format with the present benchmarks. However, within multiple-choice tasks, format (two-way vs.\\ four-way) does not predict $\\alpha$ (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}), suggesting that format alone does not drive the observed variation.\n\nAll evaluations use lm-evaluation-harness v0.4.x \\citep{gao2024lmeval} with default prompts and greedy decoding; accuracy serves as the primary metric for all benchmarks. Bootstrap analyses use random seed 42 throughout.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_method_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Experimental framework. For each architecture, we compare the pure-recurrent baseline against its hybrid (+attention) variant, classifying every evaluation example as helped, hurt, or unchanged. Cross-architecture overlap of these per-example effect sets is measured via pairwise Jaccard similarity ($J \\approx 0.10$), revealing architecture-specific sensitivity patterns.}\n\\label{fig:method_overview}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Convergence Metrics}\\label{ssec:convergence_metrics}\n\nWe quantify cross-architecture agreement as a function of attention ratio. For each benchmark and attention ratio, we compute the cross-architecture standard deviation $\\sigma$ over the three architectures' accuracy scores. To measure the relationship between attention and convergence, we compute the Spearman rank correlation $\\rho$ between attention ratio rank and $\\sigma$ across the five attention configurations ($n = 5$). A negative $\\rho$ indicates that higher attention ratios are associated with lower cross-architecture variance.\n\nWe also report the convergence ratio $\\sigma_{\\text{pure}} / \\sigma_{\\text{h3-1}}$, defined as the standard deviation under the pure-recurrent configuration divided by that under the highest attention ratio (25\\%). Values greater than 1 indicate that higher attention ratios are associated with lower cross-architecture variance.\n\n\\subsection{Per-Example Decomposition}\\label{ssec:per_example_decomposition}\n\nWe decompose performance changes at the individual-example level on four benchmarks spanning three task categories: LAMBADA (5{,}153 examples; retrieval), HellaSwag (10{,}042; reasoning), PIQA (1{,}838; reasoning, two-choice), and ARC-Easy (2{,}376; mixed). HellaSwag uses normalized accuracy (acc\\_norm) as the per-example correctness criterion. Supplementary analyses on BoolQ (3{,}270) and WinoGrande (1{,}267) appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq} and \\ref{app:winogrande}. For each architecture, we compare the pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid (25\\% attention), which maximizes the recurrent--hybrid contrast; \\S\\ref{ssec:per_example} confirms that $\\alpha$ is stable across intermediate ratios (variation $< 0.03$). Each example is classified as:\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Helped}: correct in the hybrid but incorrect in the pure model.\n    \\item \\textbf{Hurt}: correct in the pure model but incorrect in the hybrid.\n    \\item \\textbf{Unchanged}: both models agree (both correct or both incorrect).\n\\end{itemize}\n\nThe net effect for each architecture is $|\\text{helped}| - |\\text{hurt}|$. A positive net effect means more examples transition to correct than to incorrect when attention is added; a negative net effect means the opposite.\n\nTo measure cross-architecture overlap, we compute the pairwise Jaccard similarity $J = |A \\cap B| / |A \\cup B|$ between the helped sets of two architectures and, separately, between their hurt sets. $J = 1$ indicates identical sets; $J = 0$ indicates no overlap. On $K$-choice tasks, binarization collapses $K{-}1$ wrong answers into a single ``incorrect'' category, potentially counting architectures as agreeing (both wrong) when they select different wrong answers. This conservative bias strengthens the multiplicity claim: true divergence may exceed what binary Jaccard captures. Appendix~\\ref{app:continuous_jaccard} confirms robustness under continuous Jaccard similarity.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Methods}\\label{ssec:statistical_methods}\n\nAll tests are two-tailed. Spearman $\\rho$ uses exact permutation tests ($5! = 120$ permutations); with $n = 5$, only $|\\rho| = 1.0$ achieves $p < .05$ ($p = .017$), so all Spearman-based inferences are descriptive and exploratory. Bonferroni correction spans all 10 benchmarks (adjusted threshold $\\alpha / 10 = 0.005$). Bootstrap 95\\% bias-corrected and accelerated (BCa) confidence intervals use 10{,}000 resamples; with $n = 3$ architectures, these are heuristic indicators of effect direction. Within-architecture comparisons use Mann-Whitney U; cross-architecture comparisons use Kruskal-Wallis. Attention weight analysis extracts per-layer cosine similarity and Shannon entropy via forward hooks (Appendix~\\ref{app:attention_patterns}). The permutation null does not condition on item difficulty; if difficulty-driven correlations inflate observed Jaccard while permutation destroys this structure, the test may be anti-conservative. However, the ${\\sim}2.5\\times$ observed-to-null ratio (all $z > 5.9$) provides substantial margin against this bias.\n",
        "numLines": 73,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 73
      }
    },

  },
```

```json
        "content": "\\vspace{-2pt}\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\nWe have shown that hybrid linear recurrent architectures which appear interchangeable by aggregate benchmarks diverge substantially at the per-example level. This multiplicity varies by task type ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) rather than attention ratio. Per-example overlap appears to stabilize at 4\\% attention (pairwise Jaccard $\\leq 0.10$); two interpretations---architecture-specific fingerprints or rapid convergence toward Transformer-like behavior---remain open (Appendix~\\ref{app:transformer_entrainment}). Confidence margins partially predict which examples are affected (AUC 0.70--0.74), with rescue thresholds architecture-invariant but disruption thresholds varying 47\\%.\n\nAt the 340M--1.3B scales studied here, per-example audit before architecture substitution is advisable when multiplicity is substantial---aggregate equivalence does not guarantee behavioral equivalence. Low cross-architecture Jaccard creates complementarity ($+7$--$14$\\,pp oracle gain), though preliminary routing experiments show exploitation remains task-dependent ($+5.2$\\,pp on LAMBADA; $-0.49$\\,pp on PIQA).\n",
        "numLines": 7,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 7
      }
```

```text
395	\end{table}
396	
397	Adjacent ratio pairs (e.g., h24-1 vs.\ h12-1) do not exhibit systematically higher Jaccard than non-adjacent pairs (e.g., h24-1 vs.\ h3-1). Across all architectures, the mean Jaccard for adjacent pairs is 0.353 (helped) and 0.311 (hurt), compared with 0.343 (helped) and 0.310 (hurt) for non-adjacent pairs. The negligible difference indicates that the mapping from attention ratio to per-example effects is non-smooth: doubling the attention ratio does not produce a proportionally more similar set of affected examples.
398	
399	\section{Cross-Benchmark Jaccard Trajectories}\label{app:cross_bench_jaccard}
400	
401	To test whether Jaccard saturation is specific to LAMBADA or a general property, we compute mean pairwise Jaccard trajectories across all four hybrid attention ratios on all four main benchmarks. Table~\ref{tab:cross_bench_jaccard} reports the results. On every benchmark, Jaccard shows no statistically significant trend with attention ratio (all $\rho \leq 0.80$, $p \geq 0.333$), with values uniformly between 0.08 and 0.11. The saturation at the first attention level observed on LAMBADA (\S\ref{ssec:decoupling}) thus generalizes: once any attention is present, each architecture's per-example response is largely fixed regardless of attention ratio.
402	
403	\begin{table}[ht]
404	\centering
405	\small
406	\caption{Mean pairwise Jaccard (mean of helped and hurt sets, 3 architecture pairs) across attention ratios and benchmarks (1.3B scale). $\rho$: Spearman correlation between attention ratio and $\bar{J}$. All $p \geq 0.333$.}
407	\label{tab:cross_bench_jaccard}
408	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
409	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccc@{}}
```

_source line 2785 · 2026-05-24T23:32:56.278Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01KBYZn72ep4gDQp5uKtAHXf`</summary>

```text
106	\caption{Jaccard hierarchy on LAMBADA (1.3B). Cross-architecture overlap ($\bar{J} \approx 0.09$) is $2\times$ the independence baseline but only one-third of within-architecture overlap ($\bar{J} \approx 0.33$). Pythia 70M within-seed Jaccard (different scale) falls at a comparable level. Error bars show the helped--hurt range.}
107	\label{fig:jaccard_hierarchy}
108	\end{figure}
109	
110	Two interpretations are consistent with the observed stability. \textbf{(a) Fingerprint view:} each recurrent backbone imposes a characteristic inductive bias that determines which examples attention can rescue, locking in architecture-specific sensitivity patterns. \textbf{(b) Convergence view:} even minimal attention causes all architectures to rapidly adopt Transformer-like behavior, with residual per-example divergence arising from incomplete convergence. Appendix~\ref{app:transformer_entrainment} shows that h3-1 correct sets indeed shift toward the Transformer baseline, providing partial support for interpretation (b). Disentangling these accounts requires ablation of the attention mechanism itself---for example, replacing learned attention weights with random projections---an important direction for future work. Attention weight analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:attention_patterns}) shows GDN and RetNet produce nearly identical attention distributions (cosine 0.995) yet differ in per-example effects, suggesting these originate in the recurrent layers. The four-benchmark variation (Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}) is consistent with independence: LAMBADA ($\alpha = 0.30$) is the only benchmark showing a variance-reduction trend despite having the highest multiplicity, while PIQA ($\alpha = 0.14$) and HellaSwag ($\alpha = 0.15$) show no variance reduction with the lowest multiplicity.
111	
112	Cross-scale analysis reveals a clear dissociation: per-example Jaccard is consistent across both scales ($\bar{J} = 0.08$--$0.10$ at 340M and 1.3B), whereas the aggregate variance trend is observed only at 1.3B on LAMBADA ($\rho = -0.50$, $p = .45$ at 340M). Ambiguity $\alpha$ is systematically higher at 340M ($\Delta \leq 8$\,pp), suggesting that the \emph{pattern} of per-example multiplicity is stable across scales while its magnitude co-varies with model capacity (Appendix~\ref{app:scale_per_example}--\ref{app:cross_scale}).
113	\vspace{-2pt}
114	\subsection{Robustness}\label{ssec:robustness}
115	
116	Removing GDN weakens the LAMBADA variance reduction ($\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$) and reverses MMLU's ($\rho = +0.6$), both driven by GDN's 16-head configuration (vs.\ 32 in others; Appendix~\ref{app:gdn_confound}). The RetNet--HGRN2 pair preserves the independence pattern ($\sigma \leq 0.008$, $J = 0.086$--$0.108$). The aggregate variance pattern therefore depends on GDN inclusion; the per-example stability pattern does not. Five-architecture validation at 340M (adding GLA and DeltaNet) confirms $J \leq 0.14$ across all ten pairwise comparisons and four benchmarks (all $p < .01$; Figure~\ref{fig:jaccard_heatmap_5arch}; Appendix~\ref{app:extended_arch}), extending the per-example divergence finding to architectures not used in the primary analysis. Full sensitivity analyses appear in Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}; Transformer baseline comparison in Appendix~\ref{app:transformer_entrainment}.
117	
```

</details>

```json
          " ",
          " \\section{Cross-Benchmark Jaccard Trajectories}\\label{app:cross_bench_jaccard}",
          " ",
          "-To test whether Jaccard saturation is specific to LAMBADA or a general property, we compute mean pairwise Jaccard trajectories across all four hybrid attention ratios on all four main benchmarks. Table~\\ref{tab:cross_bench_jaccard} reports the results. On every benchmark, Jaccard shows no statistically significant trend with attention ratio (all $\\rho \\leq 0.80$, $p \\geq 0.333$), with values uniformly between 0.08 and 0.11. The saturation at the first attention level observed on LAMBADA (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}) thus generalizes: once any attention is present, each architecture's per-example response is largely fixed regardless of attention ratio.",
          "+To test whether the Jaccard stability pattern is specific to LAMBADA or a general property, we compute mean pairwise Jaccard trajectories across all four hybrid attention ratios on all four main benchmarks. Table~\\ref{tab:cross_bench_jaccard} reports the results. On every benchmark, Jaccard shows no statistically significant trend with attention ratio (all $\\rho \\leq 0.80$, $p \\geq 0.333$), with values uniformly between 0.08 and 0.11. The stability observed at the first attention level on LAMBADA (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}) thus appears to hold across benchmarks: once any attention is present, each architecture's per-example response remains largely consistent regardless of attention ratio.",
          " ",
          " \\begin{table}[ht]",
          " \\centering"
        ]
      }
```

```json
          " ",
          " Two interpretations are consistent with the observed stability. \\textbf{(a) Fingerprint view:} each recurrent backbone imposes a characteristic inductive bias that determines which examples attention can rescue, locking in architecture-specific sensitivity patterns. \\textbf{(b) Convergence view:} even minimal attention causes all architectures to rapidly adopt Transformer-like behavior, with residual per-example divergence arising from incomplete convergence. Appendix~\\ref{app:transformer_entrainment} shows that h3-1 correct sets indeed shift toward the Transformer baseline, providing partial support for interpretation (b). Disentangling these accounts requires ablation of the attention mechanism itself---for example, replacing learned attention weights with random projections---an important direction for future work. Attention weight analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:attention_patterns}) shows GDN and RetNet produce nearly identical attention distributions (cosine 0.995) yet differ in per-example effects, suggesting these originate in the recurrent layers. The four-benchmark variation (Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}) is consistent with independence: LAMBADA ($\\alpha = 0.30$) is the only benchmark showing a variance-reduction trend despite having the highest multiplicity, while PIQA ($\\alpha = 0.14$) and HellaSwag ($\\alpha = 0.15$) show no variance reduction with the lowest multiplicity.",
          " ",
          "-Cross-scale analysis reveals a clear dissociation: per-example Jaccard is consistent across both scales ($\\bar{J} = 0.08$--$0.10$ at 340M and 1.3B), whereas the aggregate variance trend is observed only at 1.3B on LAMBADA ($\\rho = -0.50$, $p = .45$ at 340M). Ambiguity $\\alpha$ is systematically higher at 340M ($\\Delta \\leq 8$\\,pp), suggesting that the \\emph{pattern} of per-example multiplicity is stable across scales while its magnitude co-varies with model capacity (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_per_example}--\\ref{app:cross_scale}).",
          "+Cross-scale analysis reveals a clear dissociation: per-example Jaccard is consistent across both scales ($\\bar{J} = 0.08$--$0.10$ at 340M and 1.3B), whereas the aggregate variance trend is observed only at 1.3B on LAMBADA ($\\rho = -0.50$, $p = .45$ at 340M). Ambiguity $\\alpha$ is systematically higher at 340M ($\\Delta \\leq 8$\\,pp), suggesting that the \\emph{pattern} of per-example multiplicity is stable across scales while its magnitude tracks model capacity (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_per_example}--\\ref{app:cross_scale}).",
```

_source line 2805 · 2026-05-24T23:33:28.833Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_013HYKXZhdFuVD2dFfkruDD8`</summary>

```text
37	
38	\begin{abstract}
39	Hybrid linear recurrent architectures that appear interchangeable by aggregate benchmarks can differ on which specific examples they handle correctly. Using a controlled model zoo \citep{wang2025hybrid} (three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales), we decompose per-example effects of adding attention and find structured predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive}: pairwise overlap of helped and hurt example sets is uniformly low (Jaccard $\approx 0.10$, roughly twice the independence baseline; all $p < .01$ across five architectures). This multiplicity varies by task type or evaluation format ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) rather than attention ratio. Per-example overlap appears to stabilize at 4\% attention and remains flat across higher ratios, suggesting that minimal attention may establish which examples each architecture affects. \citet{yang2026benchmark} document analogous per-item disagreement (16--66\%) among frontier large language models (LLMs) despite comparable aggregate scores; our within-family design isolates backbone variation from training-regime differences. Confidence margins partially predict affected examples (area under the ROC curve [AUC] 0.70--0.74). Low cross-architecture overlap implies substantial complementarity, though routing-based exploitation remains task-dependent.
40	\end{abstract}
41	
```

```text
Links: [{"title":"[2602.11898] Benchmark Illusion: Disagreement among LLMs and Its Scientific Consequences","url":"https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.11898"},{"title":"[2602.11898v1] Benchmark Illusion: Disagreement among LLMs and Its Scientific Consequences","url":"https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.11898v1"},{"title":"Computation and Language","url":"https://www.arxiv.org/list/cs.CL/pastweek?skip=4&show=500"},{"title":"(PDF) Benchmark Illusion: Disagreement among LLMs and Its Scientific Consequences","url":"https://www.researchgate.net/publication/400742393_Benchmark_Illusion_Disagreement_among_LLMs_and_Its_Scientific_Consequences"},{"title":"Benchmark Illusion: Disagreement among LLMs and Its Scientific Consequences","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2602.11898"},{"title":"Benchmark Illusion: Disagreement among LLMs and Its Scientific Consequences","url":"https://arxiv.org/html/2602.11898"},{"title":"The Illusion of Stochasticity in LLMs","url":"https://arxiv.org/html/2604.06543v1"}]

I found the paper you're looking for! Here's the information about arXiv:2602.11898:

**Title:** "Benchmark Illusion: Disagreement among LLMs and Its Scientific Consequences"

**Authors:** Eddie Yang and Dashun Wang

**Key Findings:**

The paper reveals that apparent convergence in benchmark accuracy can conceal deep epistemic divergence. Using two major reasoning benchmarks - MMLU-Pro and GPQA - the researchers show that LLMs achieving comparable accuracy still disagree on 16-66% of items, and 16-38% among top-performing frontier models.

When such models are used for scientific data annotation and inference, their hidden disagreements propagate into research results: in re-analyses of published studies in education and political science, switching the annotation model can change estimated treatment effects by more than 80%, and in some cases reverses their sign.

**Main Conclusion:** The findings illustrate a benchmark illusion, where equal accuracy may conceal disagreement, with model choice becoming a hidden yet consequential variable for scientific reproducibility.

The paper was submitted on February 12, 2026.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.
```
